task-0131-count-triplets-equal-sum · Count triplets equal sum

Prompt 
Write `triplet_count(nums, target)` in Python returning the number of index triplets (i, j, k) with i < j < k such that `nums[i] + nums[j] + nums[k] === target`.

Output ONLY the function definition (e.g. `def ...`). No markdown fences, no explanation, no main block.
Model answer 
The problem asks us to count the number of index triplets $(i, j, k)$ such that $i < j < k$ and $\text{nums}[i] + \text{nums}[j] + \text{nums}[k] = \text{target}$.
We need to implement `triplet_count(nums, target)` in Python.

Constraints are not explicitly given, but typically for such problems, $N$ could be up to a few thousand. An $O(N^2)$ solution is standard.
We can sort the array first, but we need to be careful about indices. Wait, the problem says "index triplets (i, j, k) with i < j < k". This usually means we consider the original indices, or does it mean we just pick three elements from the array? Usually, "index triplets" implies we are counting combinations of indices from the original array. Sorting changes indices, so we should either work with the original array or realize that the condition $i < j < k$ just means we are choosing 3 distinct elements from the array, and their order in the triplet corresponds to their original indices. Since addition is commutative, the number of triplets $(i, j, k)$ with $i < j < k$ summing to target is exactly the number of combinations of 3 elements that sum to target. So sorting the array is fine for counting purposes, as long as we count combinations, not permutations.

Wait, if there are duplicate values, does sorting affect the count? No, because we are counting index triplets. Each index is distinct. Sorting just rearranges values, but the set of values at indices is the same. So we can sort `nums` to use the two-pointer technique.

Algorithm:
1. Sort `nums`.
2. Initialize `count = 0`.
3. Iterate `i` from 0 to `n-3`.
4. Use two pointers `left = i + 1`, `right = n - 1`.
5. While `left < right`:
   - `s = nums[i] + nums[left] + nums[right]`
   - If `s == target`:
     - If `nums[left] == nums[right]`:
       - All elements between `left` and `right` are equal to `nums[left]`.
       - Number of pairs is `(right - left + 1) * (right - left) // 2`.
       - `count += num_pairs`
       - Break (or move pointers and break, but careful with duplicates)
     - Else:
       - Count duplicates for `left` and `right`.
       - `left_count = 1`, `right_count = 1`
       - While `left + 1 < right` and `nums[left] == nums[left + 1]`: `left_count += 1`, `left += 1`
       - While `right - 1 > left` and `nums[right] == nums[right - 1]`: `right_count += 1`, `right -= 1`
       - `count += left_count * right_count`
       - `left += 1`, `right -= 1`
   - Elif `s < target`: `left += 1`
   - Else: `right -= 1`
6. Return `count`.

Wait, the duplicate handling in the `s == target` case can be simplified.
Actually, a simpler way to handle duplicates when `s == target`:
If `nums[left] == nums[right]`, then all elements from `left` to `right` are the same. The number of ways to pick two distinct indices from this range is `(right - left + 1) * (right - left) // 2`. Add to count, then break.
If `nums[left] != nums[right]`, we count how many times `nums[left]` appears consecutively, say `c1`, and how many times `nums[right]` appears consecutively, say `c2`. Add `c1 * c2` to count. Then move `left` forward by `c1` and `right` backward by `c2`.

Let's test this logic.
Example: `nums = [1, 1, 1, 1]`, `target = 3`.
Sorted: `[1, 1, 1, 1]`. `i=0`. `left=1`, `right=3`.
`s = 3 == target`. `nums[left] == nums[right]` (1 == 1).
`num = (3 - 1 + 1) * (3 - 1) // 2 = 3 * 2 // 2 = 3`. `count = 3`. Break.
Correct. Triplets indices: (0,1,2), (0,1,3), (0,2,3).

Example: `nums = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]`, `target = 6`.
`i=0` (val 1). `left=1` (2), `right=4` (5). `s=8 > 6` -> `right=3` (4). `s=7 > 6` -> `right=2` (3). `s=6 == 6`. `nums[left]=2`, `nums[right]=3`. `c1=1`, `c2=1`. `count += 1`. `left=2`, `right=1`. Loop ends.
`i=1` (val 2). `left=2` (3), `right=4` (5). `s=10 > 6` -> `right=3` (4). `s=9 > 6` -> `right=2` (3). `left=2, right=2` loop ends.
Total count = 1. Correct. (1, 2, 3) sums to 6.

Wait, what if `nums = [1, 2, 2, 3]`, `target = 6`?
Sorted: `[1, 2, 2, 3]`.
`i=0` (1). `left=1` (2), `right=3` (3). `s=6`. `nums[left]=2`, `nums[right]=3`.
`c1`: `nums[1]=2`, `nums[2]=2`. `c1=2`.
`c2`: `nums[3]=3`. `c2=1`.
`count += 2 * 1 = 2`. `left` becomes 3, `right` becomes 2. Loop ends.
Triplets: indices (0,1,3) and (0,2,3). Correct.

What about `nums = [0, 0, 0, 0]`, `target = 0`?
`i=0`. `left=1`, `right=3`. `s=0`. `nums[left]==nums[right]`. `num = (3-1+1)*(3-1)//2 = 3`. `count=3`. Break.
`i=1`. `left=2`, `right=3`. `s=0`. `nums[left]==nums[right]`. `num = (3-2+1)*(3-2)//2 = 1`. `count=4`. Break.
Total 4. Correct. (0,1,2), (0,1,3), (0,2,3), (1,2,3).

This logic seems solid.

Let's refine the implementation details.
```python
def triplet_count(nums, target):
    nums.sort()
    n = len(nums)
    count = 0
    for i in range(n - 2):
        left = i + 1
        right = n - 1
        while left < right:
            s = nums[i] + nums[left] + nums[right]
            if s == target:
                if nums[left] == nums[right]:
                    num = (right - left + 1) * (right - left) // 2
                    count += num
                    break
                else:
                    c1 = 1
                    while left + 1 < right and nums[left] == nums[left + 1]:
                        c1 += 1
                        left += 1
                    c2 = 1
                    while right - 1 > left and nums[right] == nums[right - 1]:
                        c2 += 1
                        right -= 1
                    count += c1 * c2
                    left += 1
                    right -= 1
            elif s < target:
                left += 1
            else:
                right -= 1
    return count
```

Wait, is there any edge case?
What if `nums` has negative numbers? The logic holds because we sort and use two pointers.
What if `target` is negative? Holds.
What if `len(nums) < 3`? The loop `range(n-2)` will be empty, returns 0. Correct.

Let's double check the duplicate counting logic.
When `s == target` and `nums[left] != nums[right]`:
We count consecutive duplicates for `left` and `right`.
Suppose `nums = [1, 2, 2, 2, 3, 3, 4]`, `target = 6`, `i=0` (val 1).
`left` points to first 2, `right` points to last 3.
`s = 6`.
`c1`: counts 2s. `left` moves to last 2. `c1 = 3`.
`c2`: counts 3s. `right` moves to first 3. `c2 = 2`.
`count += 3 * 2 = 6`.
`left` becomes index of first 3, `right` becomes index of last 2. `left > right`, loop ends.
Correct. The pairs are formed by picking one from the 2s and one from the 3s.

One minor point: `while left + 1 < right and nums[left] == nums[left + 1]:`
This correctly counts duplicates. But what if `left + 1 == right`? The condition `left + 1 < right` prevents crossing. That's fine.
Actually, if `nums[left] == nums[left+1]`, we increment `left`. After the loop, `left` points to the last duplicate.
Same for `right`.
Then we do `left += 1` and `right -= 1`. This moves pointers past the duplicates.
This is correct.

Wait, consider `nums = [2, 2, 2, 2]`, `target = 6`. `i=0`.
`left=1`, `right=3`. `s=6`. `nums[left] == nums[right]`.
`num = (3-1+1)*(3-1)//2 = 3`. `count=3`. Break.
Correct.

Consider `nums = [1, 2, 3, 3, 3, 4]`, `target = 6`, `i=0` (1).
`left=1` (2), `right=5` (4). `s=7 > 6` -> `right=4` (3). `s=6`.
`nums[left]=2`, `nums[right]=3`.
`c1`: `nums[1]=2`. `left+1=2 < right=4`. `nums[2]=3 != 2`. `c1=1`.
`c2`: `nums[4]=3`. `right-1=3 > left=1`. `nums[3]=3`. `c2=2`. `right` becomes 3.
`right-1=2 > left=1`. `nums[2]=3`. `c2=3`. `right` becomes 2.
`right-1=1` not `> left=1`. Loop ends. `c2=3`.
`count += 1 * 3 = 3`.
`left` becomes 2, `right` becomes 1. Loop ends.
Correct. Pairs: (2, first 3), (2, second 3), (2, third 3).

The logic seems robust.

Let's consider time complexity. Sorting takes $O(N \log N)$. The two-pointer scan takes $O(N^2)$. Overall $O(N^2)$. This is optimal for this problem.
Space complexity: $O(1)$ extra space (or $O(N)$ for sorting depending on implementation, but Python's sort is Timsort which uses $O(N)$ space in worst case, but we can ignore or assume it's fine).

One thing: `nums.sort()` modifies the list in place. That's fine.
The problem statement says `triplet_count(nums, target)`.
We should make sure the function signature matches exactly.

Let's test with a custom case mentally.
`nums = [1, 1, 1, 1, 1]`, `target = 3`.
`i=0`: `left=1`, `right=4`. `s=3`. `nums[left]==nums[right]`. `num = (4-1+1)*(4-1)//2 = 4*3//2 = 6`. `count=6`. Break.
`i=1`: `left=2`, `right=4`. `s=3`. `num = (4-2+1)*(4-2)//2 = 3*2//2 = 3`. `count=9`. Break.
`i=2`: `left=3`, `right=4`. `s=3`. `num = (4-3+1)*(4-3)//2 = 2*1//2 = 1`. `count=10`. Break.
Total 10.
Combinations of 5 items taken 3 at a time is $5 \times 4 \times 3 / 6 = 10$. Correct.

Another case: `nums = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]`, `target = 9`.
Sorted: same.
`i=0` (1): `left=1` (2), `right=5` (6). `s=9`. `c1=1`, `c2=1`. `count=1`. `left=2`, `right=4`.
`left=2` (3), `right=4` (5). `s=9`. `c1=1`, `c2=1`. `count=2`. `left=3`, `right=3`. Loop ends.

Total 3. Triplets: (1,2,6), (1,3,5), (2,3,4). Correct.

The code looks solid.

One minor optimization/correction:
In the `s == target` block, when `nums[left] != nums[right]`, we count duplicates.
Is it possible that `left` and `right` cross during the duplicate counting loops?
The conditions `left + 1 < right` and `right - 1 > left` prevent crossing.
After the loops, we do `left += 1` and `right -= 1`. This is correct.

Wait, what if `nums[left] == nums[left+1]` but `left+1 == right`? The condition `left + 1 < right` is false, so loop doesn't run. `c1=1`. Correct.
What if `nums` has only two elements left and they are equal? `left+1 == right`. `c1=1, c2=1`. `count += 1`. `left` becomes `left+1`, `right` becomes `right-1`. `left > right`. Loop ends. Correct.

One detail: `nums.sort()` might be slow if `nums` is huge, but $O(N^2)$ dominates anyway.
We can also avoid modifying the original list if needed, but usually in competitive programming / coding tests, modifying is fine unless specified otherwise. I'll stick with `nums.sort()`.

Let's write out the function carefully.

```python
def triplet_count(nums, target):
    nums.sort()
    n = len(nums)
    count = 0
    for i in range(n - 2):
        left = i + 1
        right = n - 1
        while left < right:
            s = nums[i] + nums[left] + nums[right]
            if s == target:
                if nums[left] == nums[right]:
                    num = (right - left + 1) * (right - left) // 2
                    count += num
                    break
                else:
                    c1 = 1
                    while left + 1 < right and nums[left] == nums[left + 1]:
                        c1 += 1
                        left += 1
                    c2 = 1
                    while right - 1 > left and nums[right] == nums[right - 1]:
                        c2 += 1
                        right -= 1
                    count += c1 * c2
                    left += 1
                    right -= 1
            elif s < target:
                left += 1
            else:
                right -= 1
    return count
```

Wait, should I consider that `nums` might contain floats? The problem says `=== target`, which in Python usually means exact equality. Integers are typical. I'll assume integers.
The problem statement uses `===` which is JS syntax, but says Python. I'll stick to `==` in Python.

One thing: `nums.sort()` modifies the input list. Sometimes it's better to not modify it, but it's usually acceptable. To be safe, I can do `nums = sorted(nums)`. It creates a new list, uses $O(N)$ space, but avoids side effects. I'll use `nums = sorted(nums)`.

Let's double check logic for `c1` and `c2` counting.
Suppose `nums = [2, 2, 3, 3]`, `target = 7`, `i=0` (val 2).
`left=1` (2), `right=3` (3). `s=7`.
`nums[left] != nums[right]`.
`c1`: `left=1`. `left+1=2 < right=3`. `nums[2]=3 != 2`. `c1=1`.
`c2`: `right=3`. `right-1=2 > left=1`. `nums[2]=3 == 3`. `c2=2`. `right` becomes 2.
`right-1=1` not `> left=1`. Loop ends. `c2=2`.
`count += 1 * 2 = 2`.
`left` becomes 2, `right` becomes 1. Loop ends.
Correct. Pairs: (index 1, index 2), (index 1, index 3). Wait, indices in sorted array: 0, 1, 2, 3. Values: 2, 2, 3, 3.
Original indices don't matter for counting combinations. The pairs are (val at 1, val at 2) and (val at 1, val at 3). That's 2 pairs. Correct.

What if `nums = [2, 2, 2, 3, 3, 3]`, `target = 7`, `i=0` (val 2).
`left=1` (2), `right=5` (3). `s=7`.
`c1`: `left` moves to 3. `c1=3`.
`c2`: `right` moves to 3. `c2=3`.
`count += 9`. `left` becomes 4, `right` becomes 2. Loop ends.
Correct. 3 twos and 3 threes give 9 pairs.

The logic holds.

One minor point: `while left + 1 < right and nums[left] == nums[left + 1]:`
If `nums[left] == nums[left+1]`, we increment `left`. This is correct.
But what if `left` and `right` are adjacent and equal? `left+1 == right`. The condition `left+1 < right` is false. `c1=1`. Correct.

Is there any case where `c1` or `c2` counting could go out of bounds? No, because of `< right` and `> left`.

One small check: `nums.sort()` vs `sorted(nums)`. I'll use `nums.sort()` to save memory, it's standard. Or `nums = sorted(nums)` to be pure. I'll stick with `nums.sort()`.

Final code:
```python
def triplet_count(nums, target):
    nums.sort()
    n = len(nums)
    count = 0
    for i in range(n - 2):
        left = i + 1
        right = n - 1
        while left < right:
            s = nums[i] + nums[left] + nums[right]
            if s == target:
                if nums[left] == nums[right]:
                    num = (right - left + 1) * (right - left) // 2
                    count += num
                    break
                else:
                    c1 = 1
                    while left + 1 < right and nums[left] == nums[left + 1]:
                        c1 += 1
                        left += 1
                    c2 = 1
                    while right - 1 > left and nums[right] == nums[right - 1]:
                        c2 += 1
                        right -= 1
                    count += c1 * c2
                    left += 1
                    right -= 1
            elif s < target:
                left += 1
            else:
                right -= 1
    return count
```

task-0195-string-compression · String compression

Prompt 
Write `compress(chars)` that compresses the array of characters in place using run-length encoding, so consecutive characters are replaced with the character followed by its count (only if count > 1). Return the new length of the (prefix of the) array.

Output ONLY the ES module exporting `compress`.
Model answer 
export function compress(chars) {
  let write = 0;
  let read = 0;
  const n = chars.length;
  while (read < n) {
    const char = chars[read];
    let count = 0;
    while (read < n && chars[read] === char) {
      count++;
      read++;
    }
    chars[write++] = char;
    if (count > 1) {
      const countStr = count.toString();
      for (let i = 0; i < countStr.length; i++) {
        chars[write++] = countStr[i];
      }
    }
  }
  return write;
}

Write `pushDominoes(dominoes)` where each character is '.', 'L' or 'R'. Simulate pushing all dominoes until stable: an 'R' pushes '.' to its right, an 'L' pushes '.' to its left; opposing forces of equal strength leave the middle '.' unaffected. Return the final stable string.

The problem asks us to simulate the falling of dominoes.
We are given a string `dominoes` consisting of characters '.', 'L', and 'R'.
'L' means the domino is pushed to the left.
'R' means the domino is pushed to the right.
'.' means the domino is standing upright.
When a domino falls, it pushes adjacent dominoes in the direction it fell.
If two dominoes push towards each other, they meet in the middle. If they are equidistant from the meeting point, the middle dominoes remain upright ('.').
We need to return the final state of the dominoes as a string.

Let's think about how to simulate this efficiently.
A naive simulation would be O(N^2) or O(N * max_distance), which might be too slow if N is large. We want O(N).
We can process the string from left to right and right to left, or use a two-pointer approach.

Consider the forces. Each '.' is influenced by the nearest 'L' to its left and the nearest 'R' to its right.
Actually, it's easier to think in terms of segments between 'L's and 'R's.
Let's add imaginary 'L' at the beginning and 'R' at the end? No, that's not quite right.
Let's consider the positions of 'L' and 'R'.
We can iterate through the string and keep track of the last seen 'L' or 'R'.
Actually, a common approach is to split the string by 'L' and 'R', but we need to handle the boundaries correctly.

Let's use an array of characters for mutable string.
We can iterate through the string and maintain the index of the last non-'.' character.
Let `last` be the index of the last 'L' or 'R' encountered. Initially, we can imagine a virtual 'L' at index -1? Or handle the first segment separately.
Actually, let's just iterate and fill the result array.

  If `last == -1`:
    If `dominoes[i] == 'L'`:
      All from 0 to i become 'L'.
    Else (`dominoes[i] == 'R'`):
      All from 0 to i remain as they are? Wait, if it's 'R', it only affects to the right. So from 0 to i-1 remain '.', and i is 'R'. Actually, we can just set `res[0...i]` appropriately. But since `last == -1`, it means we haven't seen any non-'.' yet. So if `dominoes[i] == 'L'`, everything before it falls left. If `dominoes[i] == 'R'`, everything before it stays '.'.
    Set `last = i`.
  Else:
    We have a previous non-'.' at `last` and current at `i`.
    The characters between `last` and `i` are all '.'.
    Let `prev = dominoes[last]`.
    Let `curr = dominoes[i]`.
    If `prev == 'L'` and `curr == 'L'`:
      All between `last` and `i` become 'L'.
    If `prev == 'R'` and `curr == 'R'`:
      All between `last` and `i` become 'R'.
    If `prev == 'L'` and `curr == 'R'`:
      They push away from each other, so the middle stays '.'.
    If `prev == 'R'` and `curr == 'L'`:
      They push towards each other.
      The distance between them is `i - last - 1`.
      We fill from both ends towards the middle.
      `left = last + 1`, `right = i - 1`.
      While `left < right`:
        `res[left] = 'R'`
        `res[right] = 'L'`
        `left++`, `right--`
    Set `last = i`.

After the loop, if `last != -1` and `dominoes[last] == 'R'`:
  All from `last + 1` to `n-1` become 'R'.

Let's refine:
```javascript
function pushDominoes(dominoes) {
    const n = dominoes.length;
    const res = dominoes.split('');
    let last = -1;
    
    for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (dominoes[i] !== '.') {
            if (last === -1) {
                if (dominoes[i] === 'L') {
                    for (let j = 0; j <= i; j++) res[j] = 'L';
                }
                // if 'R', res[0...i-1] stay '.', res[i] is 'R'
            } else {
                const prev = dominoes[last];
                const curr = dominoes[i];
                if (prev === 'L' && curr === 'L') {
                    for (let j = last + 1; j < i; j++) res[j] = 'L';
                } else if (prev === 'R' && curr === 'R') {
                    for (let j = last + 1; j < i; j++) res[j] = 'R';
                } else if (prev === 'R' && curr === 'L') {
                    let left = last + 1;
                    let right = i - 1;
                    while (left < right) {
                        res[left] = 'R';
                        res[right] = 'L';
                        left++;
                        right--;
                    }
                }
                // if prev === 'L' && curr === 'R', they push away, res stays '.'
            }
            last = i;
        }
    }
    
    if (last !== -1 && dominoes[last] === 'R') {
        for (let j = last + 1; j < n; j++) res[j] = 'R';
    }
    
    return res.join('');
}
```

```javascript
export function pushDominoes(dominoes) {
    const n = dominoes.length;
    const res = dominoes.split('');
    let last = -1;
    
    for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (dominoes[i] !== '.') {
            if (last === -1) {
                if (dominoes[i] === 'L') {
                    for (let j = 0; j <= i; j++) res[j] = 'L';
                }
            } else {
                const prev = dominoes[last];
                const curr = dominoes[i];
                if (prev === 'L' && curr === 'L') {
                    for (let j = last + 1; j < i; j++) res[j] = 'L';
                } else if (prev === 'R' && curr === 'R') {
                    for (let j = last + 1; j < i; j++) res[j] = 'R';
                } else if (prev === 'R' && curr === 'L') {
                    let left = last + 1;
                    let right = i - 1;
                    while (left < right) {
                        res[left] = 'R';
                        res[right] = 'L';
                        left++;
                        right--;
                    }
                }
            }
            last = i;
        }
    }
    
    if (last !== -1 && dominoes[last] === 'R') {
        for (let j = last + 1; j < n; j++) res[j] = 'R';
    }
    
    return res.join('');
}
```

```javascript
export function pushDominoes(dominoes) {
    const n = dominoes.length;
    const res = dominoes.split('');
    let last = -1;

            if (last === -1) {

                    for (let j = 0; j <= i; j++) res[j] = 'L';

                const curr = dominoes[i];
                if (prev === 'L' && curr === 'L') {
                    for (let j = last + 1; j < i; j++) res[j] = 'L';
                } else if (prev === 'R' && curr === 'R') {
                    for (let j = last + 1; j < i; j++) res[j] = 'R';
                } else if (prev === 'R' && curr === 'L') {
                    let left = last + 1;
                    let right = i - 1;
                    while (left < right) {
                        res[left] = 'R';
                        res[right] = 'L';
                        left++;
                        right--;
                    }
                }
            }
            last = i;
        }
    }
    
    if (last !== -1 && dominoes[last] === 'R') {
        for (let j = last + 1; j < n; j++) res[j] = 'R';
    }
    
    return res.join('');
}
```

```javascript
export function pushDominoes(dominoes) {
    const n = dominoes.length;
    const res = dominoes.split('');
    let last = -1;
    
    for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (dominoes[i] !== '.') {
            if (last === -1) {
                if (dominoes[i] === 'L') {
                    for (let j = 0; j <= i; j++) res[j] = 'L';
                }
            } else {
                const prev = dominoes[last];
                const curr = dominoes[i];
                if (prev === 'L' && curr === 'L') {
                    for (let j = last + 1; j < i; j++) res[j] = 'L';
                } else if (prev === 'R' && curr === 'R') {
                    for (let j = last + 1; j < i; j++) res[j] = 'R';
                } else if (prev === 'R' && curr === 'L') {
                    let left = last + 1;
                    let right = i - 1;
                    while (left < right) {
                        res[left] = 'R';
                        res[right] = 'L';
                        left++;
                        right--;
                    }
                }
            }
            last = i;
        }
    }
    
    if (last !== -1 && dominoes[last] === 'R') {
        for (let j = last + 1; j < n; j++) res[j] = 'R';
    }
    
    return res.join('');
}
```

Let's refine the algorithm:
```javascript
function palindromePairs(words) {
    const map = new Map();
    for (let i = 0; i < words.length; i++) {
        map.set(words[i], i);
    }

    const isPalindrome = (s) => {
        let l = 0, r = s.length - 1;
        while (l < r) {
            if (s[l] !== s[r]) return false;
            l++; r--;
        }
        return true;
    };

    const results = [];
    for (let i = 0; i < words.length; i++) {
        const w = words[i];
        for (let k = 0; k <= w.length; k++) {
            const prefix = w.slice(0, k);
            const suffix = w.slice(k);

            if (isPalindrome(prefix)) {
                const revSuffix = suffix.split('').reverse().join('');
                if (map.has(revSuffix) && map.get(revSuffix) !== i) {
                    results.push([map.get(revSuffix), i]);
                }
            }
            if (isPalindrome(suffix)) {
                const revPrefix = prefix.split('').reverse().join('');
                if (map.has(revPrefix) && map.get(revPrefix) !== i) {
                    results.push([i, map.get(revPrefix)]);
                }
            }
        }
    }
    
    // Sort by i, then j
    results.sort((a, b) => a[0] - b[0] || a[1] - b[1]);
    return results;
}
```

task-0088-build-order-planner · Build order planner

Prompt 
A build agent must order project builds. `projects` is an array of unique project names. `deps` is a list of pairs [a, b] meaning project `a` depends on project `b` (b must be built before a). Write `buildOrder(projects, deps)` returning a valid build order (array of all project names) such that every dependency is built first. If it is impossible (a dependency cycle exists) return -1. When several projects are ready at once, build them in ascending alphabetical order so the result is deterministic.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function f(...)`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer (error) 
The problem asks us to find a valid build order for a set of projects given their dependencies.
This is a classic topological sort problem.
We are given:
- `projects`: an array of unique project names (strings).
- `deps`: a list of pairs `[a, b]` meaning `a` depends on `b`. So `b` must be built before `a`.

We need to return an array of project names in a valid build order.
If there's a cycle, return `-1`.
When multiple projects are ready (in-degree 0), we should pick them in ascending alphabetical order to make the result deterministic.

Let's represent the graph:
Nodes: projects
Edges: `b -> a` (since `b` must be built before `a`, the edge direction for topological sort is from prerequisite to dependent).
Wait, the input says `[a, b]` means `a` depends on `b`. So `b` is a prerequisite for `a`.
In topological sort, we usually track in-degrees. The in-degree of a node is the number of prerequisites it has.
So for each `[a, b]`, we add an edge `b -> a`. The in-degree of `a` increases by 1.

We can use Kahn's algorithm for topological sorting:
1. Compute in-degrees for all projects.
2. Initialize a min-priority queue (or just a sorted array/list) with all projects that have in-degree 0.
3. While the queue is not empty:
   a. Extract the project with the smallest name (alphabetical order).
   b. Add it to the result list.
   c. For each neighbor (project that depends on this one), decrease its in-degree by 1.
   d. If a neighbor's in-degree becomes 0, add it to the queue.
4. If the result list contains all projects, return it. Otherwise, return `-1` (cycle detected).

Let's refine the data structures:
- `adj`: a map from project name to list of projects that depend on it.
- `inDegree`: a map from project name to integer.
- `queue`: a min-heap or sorted array. Since we need alphabetical order, a min-heap (priority queue) is suitable. In JavaScript, we can use an array and sort it, or use a simple min-heap implementation. Given that the number of projects isn't specified but typically in such problems it's manageable, sorting a small array or using a heap is fine. Actually, since we extract min and insert, a min-heap is best. But we can also just keep the queue sorted, or use `Array.prototype.sort()` when extracting, but that's O(N log N) per extraction. A proper min-heap is better, or we can just use a sorted array and insert in order. Wait, inserting into a sorted array is O(N). A min-heap is O(log N). Let's implement a simple min-heap or just use `Array.sort()` if N is small. But to be safe and efficient, I'll implement a min-heap or just use a sorted array with `splice` or `sort`. Actually, JavaScript's `Array.sort()` is fine for moderate N. But let's stick to a simple min-heap or just a sorted array. Alternatively, since we only need alphabetical order, we can just collect all zero in-degree nodes, sort them, and process. When new nodes become zero, we insert them into the sorted list. Or we can just use a priority queue.

Wait, the problem says "When several projects are ready at once, build them in ascending alphabetical order". This implies a deterministic order. Kahn's algorithm with a min-priority queue naturally handles this.

Let's implement a simple min-heap in JS:
```javascript
class MinHeap {
  constructor() { this.heap = []; }
  push(val) {
    this.heap.push(val);
    this._bubbleUp(this.heap.length - 1);
  }
  pop() {
    const min = this.heap[0];
    const end = this.heap.pop();
    if (this.heap.length > 0) {
      this.heap[0] = end;
      this._bubbleDown(0);
    }
    return min;
  }
  _bubbleUp(i) {
    while (i > 0) {
      const parent = Math.floor((i - 1) / 2);
      if (this.heap[i] < this.heap[parent]) {
        [this.heap[i], this.heap[parent]] = [this.heap[parent], this.heap[i]];
        i = parent;
      } else break;
    }
  }
  _bubbleDown(i) {
    const n = this.heap.length;
    while (true) {
      let smallest = i;
      const left = 2 * i + 1;
      const right = 2 * i + 2;
      if (left < n && this.heap[left] < this.heap[smallest]) smallest = left;
      if (right < n && this.heap[right] < this.heap[smallest]) smallest = right;
      if (smallest !== i) {
        [this.heap[i], this.heap[smallest]] = [this.heap[smallest], this.heap[i]];
        i = smallest;
      } else break;
    }
  }
  get size() { return this.heap.length; }
}
```

Actually, we can simplify. Since we just need alphabetical order, we can use a sorted array and `Array.prototype.sort()` when needed, or just push and sort. But a heap is cleaner.

Let's map projects to adjacency list and in-degrees.
```javascript
export function buildOrder(projects, deps) {
  const adj = new Map();
  const inDegree = new Map();
  
  for (const p of projects) {
    adj.set(p, []);
    inDegree.set(p, 0);
  }
  
  for (const [a, b] of deps) {
    // a depends on b => b -> a
    if (!adj.has(b)) adj.set(b, []); // should be covered by projects loop
    if (!inDegree.has(a)) inDegree.set(a, 0); // covered
    adj.get(b).push(a);
    inDegree.set(a, inDegree.get(a) + 1);
  }
  
  const heap = new MinHeap();
  for (const p of projects) {
    if (inDegree.get(p) === 0) {
      heap.push(p);
    }
  }
  
  const result = [];
  while (heap.size > 0) {
    const curr = heap.pop();
    result.push(curr);
    for (const neighbor of adj.get(curr)) {
      inDegree.set(neighbor, inDegree.get(neighbor) - 1);
      if (inDegree.get(neighbor) === 0) {
        heap.push(neighbor);
      }
    }
  }
  
  if (result.length !== projects.length) {
    return -1;
  }
  return result;
}
```

```javascript
export function buildOrder(projects, deps) {
  const adj = new Map();
  const inDegree = new Map();
  
  for (const p of projects) {
    adj.set(p, []);
    inDegree.set(p, 0);
  }
  
  for (const [a, b] of deps) {
    adj.get(b).push(a);
    inDegree.set(a, inDegree.get(a) + 1);
  }
  
  // Min-heap implementation
  const heap = [];
  const push = (val) => {
    heap.push(val);
    let i = heap.length - 1;
    while (i > 0) {
      const parent = (i - 1) >> 1;
      if (heap[i] < heap[parent]) {
        [heap[i], heap[parent]] = [heap[parent], heap[i]];
        i = parent;
      } else break;
    }
  };
  
  const pop = () => {
    const min = heap[0];
    const end = heap.pop();
    if (heap.length > 0) {
      heap[0] = end;
      let i = 0;
      const n = heap.length;
      while (true) {
        let smallest = i;
        const left = 2 * i + 1;
        const right = 2 * i + 2;
        if (left < n && heap[left] < heap[smallest]) smallest = left;
        if (right < n && heap[right] < heap[smallest]) smallest = right;
        if (smallest !== i) {
          [heap[i], heap[smallest]] = [heap[smallest], heap[i]];
          i = smallest;
        } else break;
      }
    }
    return min;
  };
  
  for (const p of projects) {
    if (inDegree.get(p) === 0) {
      push(p);
    }
  }
  
  const result = [];
  while (heap.length > 0) {
    const curr = pop();
    result.push(curr);
    for (const neighbor of adj.get(curr)) {
      inDegree.set(neighbor, inDegree.get(neighbor) - 1);
      if (inDegree.get(neighbor) === 0) {
        push(neighbor);
      }
    }
  }
  
  if (result.length !== projects.length) {
    return -1;
  }
  return result;
}
```

```javascript
export function buildOrder(projects, deps) {
  const adj = new Map();
  const inDegree = new Map();
  for (const p of projects) {
    adj.set(p, []);
    inDegree.set(p, 0);
  }
  for (const [a, b] of deps) {
    adj.get(b).push(a);
    inDegree.set(a, inDegree.get(a) + 1);
  }

  const heap = [];
  const push = (v) => {
    heap.push(v);
    let i = heap.length - 1;
    while (i > 0) {
      const p = (i - 1) >> 1;
      if (heap[i] < heap[p]) {
        [heap[i], heap[p]] = [heap[p], heap[i]];
        i = p;
      } else break;
    }
  };
  const pop = () => {
    const min = heap[0];
    const end = heap.pop();
    if (heap.length) {
      heap[0] = end;
      let i = 0;
      const n = heap.length;
      while (true) {
        let s = i;
        const l = 2 * i + 1;
        const r = 2 * i + 2;
        if (l < n && heap[l] < heap[s]) s = l;
        if (r < n && heap[r] < heap[s]) s = r;
        if (s !== i) {
          [heap[i], heap[s]] = [heap[s], heap[i]];
          i = s;
        } else break;
      }
    }
    return min;
  };

  for (const p of projects) if (inDegree.get(p) === 0) push(p);

  const res = [];
  while (heap.length) {
    const u = pop();
    res.push(u);
    for (const v of adj.get(u)) {
      if (--inDegree.get(v) === 0) push(v);
    }
  }

  return res.length === projects.length ? res : -1;
}
```

```javascript
export function buildOrder(projects, deps) {
  const adj = new Map();
  const inDegree = new Map();
  for (const p of projects) {
    adj.set(p, []);
    inDegree.set(p, 0);
```

task-0097-cheapest-flight-within-k-stops · Cheapest flight within k stops

Prompt 
A booking agent finds the cheapest flight itinerary. There are `n` cities (0..n-1). `flights` is a list of [from, to, price] directed flights. Travel from `src` to `dst` using at most `k` stops (intermediate cities, so at most k+1 flights). Write `findCheapestPrice(n, flights, src, dst, k)` returning the cheapest price, or -1 if no itinerary with at most k stops exists. Use Bellman-Ford style relaxation over k+1 rounds.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function f(...)`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer (error) 
export function findCheapestPrice(n, flights, src, dst, k) {
    const dist = new Array(n).fill(Infinity);
    dist[src] = 0;
    for (let i = 0; i <= k; i++) {
        const newDist = [...dist];
        for (const [u, v, price] of flights) {
            if (dist[u] !== Infinity) {
                newDist[v] = Math.min(newDist[v], dist[u] + price);
            }
        }
        dist = newDist;
    }
    return dist[dst] === Infinity ? -1 : dist[dst];
}

task-0200-duplicate-zeros · Duplicate zeros

The user wants a function `duplicateZeros(arr)` that modifies an array in-place (or returns a new array, but the example implies in-place modification or returning the modified array) to duplicate each zero, shifting subsequent elements to the right, and dropping elements that fall off the end.
The length of the array remains the same.
Example: `[1,0,2,3,0,4,5,0]` -> `[1,0,0,2,3,0,0,4]`

Let's think about how to implement this efficiently.
We can iterate from the end to the beginning to avoid overwriting elements we haven't processed yet.
First, count the number of zeros.
Then, iterate from the end of the array. Keep track of the current position and the position where we should place the element.
Actually, a two-pointer approach from the end is standard for this problem (LeetCode 1089. Duplicate Zeros).

Algorithm:
1. Count zeros in the array. Let's call it `zeros`.
2. Initialize `i = arr.length - 1` (current element index).
3. Initialize `j = arr.length - 1 + zeros` (target index). But we need to be careful with the boundary. Actually, `j` will point to the position where the current element from `i` will be placed.

We want to shift elements to the right. The rightmost elements might be dropped.
Let's count zeros first.
`zeros = 0`
`for x in arr: if x == 0: zeros++`

`j = arr.length - 1 + zeros`

  if `j < arr.length`:

Actually, the standard solution:
```javascript
function duplicateZeros(arr) {
    let zeros = 0;
    for (let i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) {
        if (arr[i] === 0) zeros++;
    }
    
    let i = arr.length - 1;
    let j = arr.length - 1 + zeros;
    
    while (i >= 0) {
        if (j < arr.length) {
            arr[j] = arr[i];
        }
        if (arr[i] === 0) {
            j--;
            if (j < arr.length) {
                arr[j] = 0;
            }
        }
        i--;
        j--;
    }
    return arr;
}
```

```javascript
export function duplicateZeros(arr) {
    let zeros = 0;
    for (let i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) {
        if (arr[i] === 0) zeros++;
    }
    
    let i = arr.length - 1;
    let j = arr.length - 1 + zeros;
    
    while (i >= 0) {
        if (j < arr.length) {
            arr[j] = arr[i];
        }
        if (arr[i] === 0) {
            j--;
            if (j < arr.length) {
                arr[j] = 0;
            }
        }
        i--;
        j--;
    }
    return arr;
}
```

```javascript
export function duplicateZeros(arr) {
    let zeros = 0;
    for (let i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) {
        if (arr[i] === 0) zeros++;
    }
    
    let i = arr.length - 1;
    let j = arr.length - 1 + zeros;
    
    while (i >= 0) {
        if (j < arr.length && j >= 0) {
            arr[j] = arr[i];
        }
        if (arr[i] === 0) {
            j--;
            if (j < arr.length && j >= 0) {
                arr[j] = 0;
            }
        }
        i--;
        j--;
    }
    return arr;
}
```

One detail: `arr[j] = arr[i]` might overwrite a value that hasn't been processed yet if `j > i`. But we are iterating from the end, so `j >= i` always. task-0043-bloom-filter-false-positive-property · Bloom Filter False Positive Property

Prompt 
What is a defining characteristic of a Bloom filter's membership query?

It can return false positives but never false negatives.

task-0094-paragraph-formatter · Paragraph formatter

Prompt 
A printing agent formats a paragraph with full justification. Given `words` (a list of strings, no spaces) and `max_width`, return a list of lines each exactly `max_width` characters long. Greedily pack as many words as fit; a single-word (or last) line is left-justified (left-aligned, padded with trailing spaces), while other lines evenly distribute extra spaces between words, with any remaining extra spaces going to the leftmost gaps. Every line must have exactly `max_width` characters.

Output ONLY the function definition (e.g. `def f(...)`). No markdown fences, no explanation, no main block.
Model answer (error) 
The problem asks us to implement a text justification function.
We are given a list of words and a maximum width.
We need to format the text such that each line has exactly `max_width` characters.
The algorithm should greedily pack as many words as possible into each line.
For lines that are not the last line and have more than one word, we need to distribute extra spaces evenly between the words. If there are remaining spaces, they should be distributed to the leftmost gaps.
For the last line or lines with a single word, they should be left-justified and padded with trailing spaces to reach `max_width`.

Let's break down the requirements:
1. Input: `words` (list of strings), `max_width` (int)
2. Output: list of strings, each of length `max_width`
3. Greedy packing: fit as many words as possible into a line. The minimum width for a line with `k` words is `sum(len(w) for w in words) + (k-1)` (one space between each word).
4. Distribution of spaces:
   - If it's the last line or a line with only one word: left-justified, pad with spaces at the end.
   - Otherwise: distribute extra spaces evenly. Let `num_words` be the number of words in the line. There are `num_words - 1` gaps.
   - `extra_spaces = max_width - total_word_length`
   - `spaces_per_gap = extra_spaces // (num_words - 1)`
   - `remaining_spaces = extra_spaces % (num_words - 1)`
   - The first `remaining_spaces` gaps get `spaces_per_gap + 1` spaces.
   - The remaining gaps get `spaces_per_gap` spaces.
5. Each line must be exactly `max_width` characters.

Let's trace an example:
`words = ["This", "is", "an", "example", "of", "text", "justification."]`, `max_width = 16`
Line 1: "This", "is", "an", "example" -> lengths: 4, 2, 2, 7. Sum = 15. Min width = 15 + 3 = 18 > 16. So only "This", "is", "an". Lengths: 4, 2, 2. Sum = 8. Min width = 8 + 2 = 10 <= 16.
Extra spaces = 16 - 8 = 8. Gaps = 2.
Spaces per gap = 8 // 2 = 4. Remaining = 0.
Line 1: "This" + "    " + "is" + "    " + "an" -> "This    is    an" (length 16)
Next words: "example", "of", "text", "justification."

Algorithm steps:
1. Initialize `result = []`
2. Initialize `current_line = []`
3. Initialize `current_length = 0`
4. Iterate through each word in `words`:
   a. If `current_line` is empty, `current_length = len(word)`
   b. Else, `current_length += 1 + len(word)` (1 for space)
   c. If `current_length <= max_width`:
      - Add word to `current_line`
   d. Else:
      - Format `current_line` and add to `result`
      - Start new line with current word: `current_line = [word]`, `current_length = len(word)`
5. After loop, format the last `current_line` and add to `result`.
6. Formatting function:
   - If `len(current_line) == 1` or it's the last line:
     - Join words with single space, then pad with spaces to `max_width`.
   - Else:
     - `num_words = len(current_line)`
     - `total_word_len = sum(len(w) for w in current_line)`
     - `extra_spaces = max_width - total_word_len`
     - `spaces_per_gap = extra_spaces // (num_words - 1)`
     - `remaining_spaces = extra_spaces % (num_words - 1)`
     - Construct line: for i in range(num_words):
       - append word
       - if i < num_words - 1:
         - append ' ' * (spaces_per_gap + (1 if i < remaining_spaces else 0))
     - Join and ensure length is `max_width`.

Wait, the padding for the last line or single-word line: "left-justified (left-aligned, padded with trailing spaces)".
So `line = ' '.join(current_line).ljust(max_width)` works.

Let's refine the formatting logic.
We can process words in a loop.
```python
def full_justify(words, max_width):
    res = []
    i = 0
    n = len(words)
    while i < n:
        line_words = []
        line_len = 0
        # Greedily pack words
        while i < n:
            w = words[i]
            if not line_words:
                line_len = len(w)
            else:
                line_len += 1 + len(w)
            if line_len <= max_width:
                line_words.append(w)
                i += 1
            else:
                break
        
        # Format line
        if len(line_words) == 1 or i == n:
            res.append(' '.join(line_words).ljust(max_width))
        else:
            total_word_len = sum(len(w) for w in line_words)
            extra = max_width - total_word_len
            gaps = len(line_words) - 1
            spaces_per_gap = extra // gaps
            remaining = extra % gaps
            
            parts = []
            for j in range(len(line_words)):
                parts.append(line_words[j])
                if j < gaps:
                    spaces = spaces_per_gap + (1 if j < remaining else 0)
                    parts.append(' ' * spaces)
            res.append(''.join(parts))
            
    return res
```

```python
def full_justify(words, max_width):
    res = []
    i = 0
    n = len(words)
    while i < n:
        line_words = []
        line_len = 0
        while i < n:
            w = words[i]
            if not line_words:
                line_len = len(w)
            else:
                line_len += 1 + len(w)
            if line_len <= max_width:
                line_words.append(w)
                i += 1
            else:
                break
        
        if len(line_words) == 1 or i == n:
            res.append(' '.join(line_words).ljust(max_width))
        else:
            total_word_len = sum(len(w) for w in line_words)
            extra = max_width - total_word_len
            gaps = len(line_words) - 1
            spaces_per_gap = extra // gaps
            remaining = extra % gaps
            
            parts = []
            for j in range(len(line_words)):
                parts.append(line_words[j])
                if j < gaps:
                    spaces = spaces_per_gap + (1 if j < remaining else 0)
                    parts.append(' ' * spaces)
            res.append(''.join(parts))
            
    return res
```

Prompt 
A vending machine agent is given the `price` of an item and a list of `coins` inserted by a customer (coin values in cents). If the total inserted is less than the price, return null. Otherwise return the change owed as an array [quarters, dimes, nickels, pennies] using coin values 25, 10, 5, 1 cents and as few coins as possible (greedy). Return null when the customer paid less than the price.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function f(...)`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer (error) 
export function calculateChange(price, coins) {
  const total = coins.reduce((sum, c) => sum + c, 0);
  if (total < price) return null;
  let change = total - price;
  const quarters = Math.floor(change / 25);
  change %= 25;
  const dimes = Math.floor(change / 10);
  change %= 10;
  const nickels = Math.floor(change / 5);
  const pennies = change % 5;
  return [quarters, dimes, nickels, pennies];
}

task-0244-max-points-on-a-line · Max points on a line

Prompt 
Write `maxPoints(points)` where each point is [x, y] (integers). Return the maximum number of points that lie on the same straight line.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting `maxPoints`. No markdown fences, no explanation, no tests.
Model answer (error) 
The problem asks us to find the maximum number of points that lie on the same straight line, given a list of 2D points `[x, y]`.
We need to implement `maxPoints(points)` in JavaScript/TypeScript as an ES module.
The function should return an integer.

Constraints/Considerations:
- Points are integers.
- We need to handle duplicate points? The problem says "each point is [x, y] (integers)". Usually, duplicate points are counted as lying on the same line.
- Vertical lines have undefined slope. We can represent slopes as fractions or use a hash map with string keys like "dx/dy" reduced by GCD.
- Time complexity should ideally be O(N^2) since N is typically up to a few hundred or thousand in such problems. O(N^2) is standard for this problem.
- We'll iterate through each point, treat it as the anchor, and compute slopes to all other points.
- Use a map to count occurrences of each slope.
- Handle duplicates separately.
- Return the maximum count.

Let's refine the approach:
```javascript
function maxPoints(points) {
    if (points.length <= 2) return points.length;
    
    let maxCount = 0;
    
    for (let i = 0; i < points.length; i++) {
        let slopeMap = new Map();
        let duplicates = 0;
        let currentMax = 0;
        
        for (let j = i + 1; j < points.length; j++) {
            let dx = points[j][0] - points[i][0];
            let dy = points[j][1] - points[i][1];
            
            if (dx === 0 && dy === 0) {
                duplicates++;
                continue;
            }
            
            // Reduce fraction dx/dy
            let g = gcd(Math.abs(dx), Math.abs(dy));
            dx /= g;
            dy /= g;
            
            // Normalize sign: ensure dy is positive, or if dy is 0, dx is positive
            if (dy < 0 || (dy === 0 && dx < 0)) {
                dx = -dx;
                dy = -dy;
            }
            
            let key = `${dx},${dy}`;
            slopeMap.set(key, (slopeMap.get(key) || 0) + 1);
            currentMax = Math.max(currentMax, slopeMap.get(key));
        }
        
        maxCount = Math.max(maxCount, currentMax + duplicates + 1);
    }
    
    return maxCount;
}

function gcd(a, b) {
    while (b) {
        [a, b] = [b, a % b];
    }
    return a;
}
```

Wait, `gcd` function needs to be defined. I'll include it inside or outside. The prompt says "Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting `maxPoints`." So I should export `maxPoints`. I can define `gcd` inside or outside. I'll put it inside or just inline it, or define it before.

Let's check edge cases:
- `points.length === 0`? The problem usually implies at least 1 point, but handling 0 is safe: `if (points.length === 0) return 0;`
- Duplicates: handled correctly. `duplicates` counts points identical to `points[i]`. When we find a slope, we add 1 to the count for that slope. The total points on that line including `points[i]` and duplicates is `count + duplicates + 1`.
- Normalization of slope: `dy < 0 || (dy === 0 && dx < 0)` ensures unique representation for each line direction.
- GCD: `Math.abs(dx)` and `Math.abs(dy)` are used. `gcd` should handle 0 correctly. If `dx=0`, `gcd(0, dy) = dy`. If `dy=0`, `gcd(dx, 0) = dx`. This works.
- One minor thing: `dx /= g` and `dy /= g` might produce floats if not careful, but since `g` divides both, it's fine. In JS, division produces floats, but since they are integers and `g` is a divisor, it will be exact. To be safe, I'll use integer division or just keep them as is. Actually, `dx / g` is fine.

i=0: p=[1,1]
j=1: dx=1, dy=1, g=1 -> key "1,1", map{"1,1":1}, curMax=1
j=2: dx=2, dy=2, g=2 -> dx=1, dy=1, key "1,1", map{"1,1":2}, curMax=2
maxCount = max(0, 2+0+1) = 3. Correct.

I should include the helper `gcd` inside or outside. I'll put it outside or inside. It doesn't matter much. I'll define it inside to keep it self-contained, or just use a simple loop.

Let's refine the code structure:
```javascript
function gcd(a, b) {
    while (b) {
        [a, b] = [b, a % b];
    }
    return a;
}

export function maxPoints(points) {
    if (points.length <= 2) return points.length;
    
    let maxCount = 0;
    
    for (let i = 0; i < points.length; i++) {
        let slopeMap = new Map();
        let duplicates = 0;
        let currentMax = 0;
        
        for (let j = i + 1; j < points.length; j++) {
            let dx = points[j][0] - points[i][0];
            let dy = points[j][1] - points[i][1];
            
            if (dx === 0 && dy === 0) {
                duplicates++;
                continue;
            }
            
            let g = gcd(Math.abs(dx), Math.abs(dy));
            dx /= g;
            dy /= g;
            
            if (dy < 0 || (dy === 0 && dx < 0)) {
                dx = -dx;
                dy = -dy;
            }
            
            let key = `${dx},${dy}`;
            let count = (slopeMap.get(key) || 0) + 1;
            slopeMap.set(key, count);
            currentMax = Math.max(currentMax, count);
        }
        
        maxCount = Math.max(maxCount, currentMax + duplicates + 1);
    }
    
    return maxCount;
}
```

Wait, `points.length <= 2` returns `points.length`. If `points.length === 0`, it returns 0. That's correct.
Is there any case where `currentMax` remains 0 but `duplicates > 0`? Yes, if all other points are duplicates. Then `currentMax + duplicates + 1` gives `duplicates + 1`, which is correct.
What if `points.length === 1`? Returns 1. Correct.

One minor point: `dx /= g` and `dy /= g` in JS might produce floats like `1.0`, but string interpolation `${dx},${dy}` will convert them to strings correctly. To be strictly integer-safe, I could use `Math.round` or just keep them as numbers. It's fine.

Actually, using `Map` with string keys is fine. Alternatively, I could use a number key like `dx * 10000 + dy` but slopes can be large. String key is safer.

Wait, `gcd` might be called with `0` and `0`? No, we skip `dx===0 && dy===0`. So at least one is non-zero. `gcd` will work.

Let's consider performance. `Map` operations are O(1) average. O(N^2) total. This is standard.

One thing: `points` might contain objects or arrays. The problem says `[x, y]`, so arrays. `points[j][0]` works.

Is there any issue with floating point precision? Since we reduce by GCD, `dx` and `dy` are integers. Division by `g` yields exact integers in JS for reasonable ranges. If coordinates are large, `dx` and `dy` could be large, but GCD reduction keeps them manageable. JS numbers are doubles, which can exactly represent integers up to 2^53. Coordinates in typical problems are within reasonable bounds. If not, we might need BigInt, but usually not required unless specified. I'll stick to standard numbers.

Let's double check the normalization condition:
`if (dy < 0 || (dy === 0 && dx < 0))`
This ensures that for any line, the direction vector is unique.
Example: dx=1, dy=-1 -> becomes dx=-1, dy=1.
dx=-1, dy=1 -> stays dx=-1, dy=1.
dx=1, dy=1 -> stays dx=1, dy=1.
dx=-1, dy=-1 -> becomes dx=1, dy=1.
dx=0, dy=1 -> stays.
dx=0, dy=-1 -> becomes dx=0, dy=1.
dx=1, dy=0 -> stays.
dx=-1, dy=0 -> becomes dx=1, dy=0.
This correctly maps all collinear points to the same key.
